I suppose… what finally made me say something is that after I eat too much, I’ve been going upstairs and making myself throw up. And then I come back down or I sort of busy myself as if I’m just tidying the kitchen, or I’ll say I’m getting ready for bed, and it’s… it’s become this awful little routine that I’m trying to hide.

I feel quite ashamed saying it out loud, honestly. I keep telling myself it’s not really happening in the way it sounds, or that I’ve got it under control, but the fact that I’m planning around it and pretending I’m doing something normal afterwards… that’s what frightened me enough to come in.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I do. It’s not just having a bit too much pudding or seconds at supper. It’s more like, if no one is in the kitchen and I know I won’t be interrupted, I can sort of… start picking, and then it becomes this whole thing.

I might go through leftovers from a church supper, then crackers, cheese, cookies, and if I’ve baked something for book club, I’ll eat that too. All in one sitting. It’s far, far more than I would ever put on a plate and serve myself as a proper meal. I wouldn’t let anyone see me eat like that. I’d be mortified. But when I’m alone, it can happen very quickly, and then I feel like I have to undo it somehow.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… not always the throwing up, no. Sometimes I don’t do that part, but I still try to sort of… compensate, I suppose.

If I’ve had one of those episodes in the evening, the next day I’ll tell myself I’m just “not hungry,” but really I’ll skip breakfast and lunch on purpose. I’ll have tea and keep busy at work or around the house so I don’t have to think about it. Or if it’s a walking-group day, I’ll push myself to do extra laps, more than I normally would, and it isn’t because I’m feeling full of energy or enjoying the walk. It’s very much because I’m thinking, I have to undo what I ate yesterday.

It sounds ridiculous when I say it, but at the time it feels almost like a rule I’ve made for myself. Like if I don’t do something the next day, then the food “counts,” if that makes sense.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. That’s exactly the frightening part of it, really. It doesn’t feel like ordinary hunger, or like I’ve made a decision to have a treat and then another one. Once I start, it’s as if a switch flips.

I can be standing there thinking, Linda, stop now, you’ve had enough, put it away, and at the very same time my hands are still reaching for more. It’s almost like I’m watching myself do it from a little distance, if that makes any sense. I know I don’t want to carry on, I know I’ll feel awful afterwards, and yet I keep opening packets or going back to the fridge or cutting another slice.

I feel embarrassed saying that because I’m a grown woman, and in every other part of my life I’m quite capable and organised. But in those moments, no, I don’t feel in control of it. I feel like I should be able to stop, but I can’t seem to make myself stop.

It’s mainly the throwing up, yes. I don’t really… I mean, the thing that finally made me say something to someone was realising I’d got into this awful little routine after eating too much.

I’ll go upstairs and make myself sick, and then come back down or sort of carry on as if I’ve just been tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed. It sounds so sneaky when I say it out loud, and I hate that, because I’m not a dishonest person generally. But I’ll make it look like I’m just doing normal evening things, putting plates away or going to brush my teeth, when actually I’ve gone off because I’m panicking about what I’ve eaten.

So yes… that’s the main way. It isn’t something I’m proud of at all.

It’s more like, if no one is in the kitchen and I know I won’t be interrupted, I can sort of… start picking, and then it becomes this whole thing.\n\nI might go through leftovers from a church supper, then crackers, cheese, cookies, and if I’ve baked something for book club, I’ll eat that too. All in one sitting. It’s far, far more than I would ever put on a plate and serve myself as a proper meal. I wouldn’t let anyone see me eat like that. I’d be mortified. But when I’m alone, it can happen very quickly, and then I feel like I have to undo it somehow.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, really… there’s hardly been a week when it hasn’t happened at least once. I mean, the throwing up specifically is often after one of those private eating spells, when I’ve eaten far more than I meant to and I panic. But sometimes I’ve tried to “make up for it” in other ways too, like not eating properly the next day or doing far too much walking, as if that somehow balances it out.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I can hear how calculated that sounds, but in the moment it feels like I’ve done something dreadful and I need to fix it. So… yes, at least weekly, since last winter.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these episodes of eating a very large amount of food in a short time without then making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Yes… not always the throwing up, no. Sometimes I don’t do that part, but I still try to sort of… compensate, I suppose.\n\nIf I’ve had one of those episodes in the evening, the next day I’ll tell myself I’m just “not hungry,” but really I’ll skip breakfast and lunch on purpose. I’ll have tea and keep busy at work or around the house so I don’t have to think about it. Or if it’s a walking-group day, I’ll push myself to do extra laps, more than I normally would, and it isn’t because I’m feeling full of energy or enjoying the walk. It’s very much because I’m thinking, I have to undo what I ate yesterday.\n\nIt sounds ridiculous when I say it, but at the time it feels almost like a rule I’ve made for myself. Like if I don’t do something the next day, then the food “counts,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can't stop eating or control how much you're eating during those episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s exactly the frightening part of it, really. It doesn’t feel like ordinary hunger, or like I’ve made a decision to have a treat and then another one. Once I start, it’s as if a switch flips.\n\nI can be standing there thinking, Linda, stop now, you’ve had enough, put it away, and at the very same time my hands are still reaching for more. It’s almost like I’m watching myself do it from a little distance, if that makes any sense. I know I don’t want to carry on, I know I’ll feel awful afterwards, and yet I keep opening packets or going back to the fridge or cutting another slice.\n\nI feel embarrassed saying that because I’m a grown woman, and in every other part of my life I’m quite capable and organised. But in those moments, no, I don’t feel in control of it. I feel like I should be able to stop, but I can’t seem to make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up the main way you try to undo the effects of eating too much, or do you use other methods, like laxatives or diuretics, as much or more often?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly the throwing up, yes. I don’t really… I mean, the thing that finally made me say something to someone was realising I’d got into this awful little routine after eating too much.\n\nI’ll go upstairs and make myself sick, and then come back down or sort of carry on as if I’ve just been tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed. It sounds so sneaky when I say it out loud, and I hate that, because I’m not a dishonest person generally. But I’ll make it look like I’m just doing normal evening things, putting plates away or going to brush my teeth, when actually I’ve gone off because I’m panicking about what I’ve eaten.\n\nSo yes… that’s the main way. It isn’t something I’m proud of at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"intent": "determine frequency of binge eating episodes without subsequent purging", "question": "How often do you have episodes of eating a very large amount of food in a short time without then making yourself throw up?"}

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Since last winter, really… I don’t think there’s been hardly any week where it hasn’t happened at least once.

I mean, not always exactly the same way each time. Sometimes it’s one of those private eating spells and then I make myself sick afterwards. Other times I don’t do that, but I’ll skip meals the next day or push myself too hard with the walking, telling myself I’m just being disciplined, when really I know I’m trying to make up for what I ate.

But if you’re asking how often it happens without me being sick afterwards specifically… it’s been fairly regular, mixed in with the other times. I suppose the honest answer is that the whole pattern has been at least weekly since the winter, one way or another.

Sometimes I don’t do that part, but I still try to sort of… compensate, I suppose.\n\nIf I’ve had one of those episodes in the evening, the next day I’ll tell myself I’m just “not hungry,” but really I’ll skip breakfast and lunch on purpose. I’ll have tea and keep busy at work or around the house so I don’t have to think about it. Or if it’s a walking-group day, I’ll push myself to do extra laps, more than I normally would, and it isn’t because I’m feeling full of energy or enjoying the walk. It’s very much because I’m thinking, I have to undo what I ate yesterday.\n\nIt sounds ridiculous when I say it, but at the time it feels almost like a rule I’ve made for myself. Like if I don’t do something the next day, then the food “counts,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can't stop eating or control how much you're eating during those episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s exactly the frightening part of it, really. It doesn’t feel like ordinary hunger, or like I’ve made a decision to have a treat and then another one. Once I start, it’s as if a switch flips.\n\nI can be standing there thinking, Linda, stop now, you’ve had enough, put it away, and at the very same time my hands are still reaching for more. It’s almost like I’m watching myself do it from a little distance, if that makes any sense. I know I don’t want to carry on, I know I’ll feel awful afterwards, and yet I keep opening packets or going back to the fridge or cutting another slice.\n\nI feel embarrassed saying that because I’m a grown woman, and in every other part of my life I’m quite capable and organised. But in those moments, no, I don’t feel in control of it. I feel like I should be able to stop, but I can’t seem to make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up the main way you try to undo the effects of eating too much, or do you use other methods, like laxatives or diuretics, as much or more often?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly the throwing up, yes. I don’t really… I mean, the thing that finally made me say something to someone was realising I’d got into this awful little routine after eating too much.\n\nI’ll go upstairs and make myself sick, and then come back down or sort of carry on as if I’ve just been tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed. It sounds so sneaky when I say it out loud, and I hate that, because I’m not a dishonest person generally. But I’ll make it look like I’m just doing normal evening things, putting plates away or going to brush my teeth, when actually I’ve gone off because I’m panicking about what I’ve eaten.\n\nSo yes… that’s the main way. It isn’t something I’m proud of at all.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes of eating a very large amount of food in a short time without then making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, really… I don’t think there’s been hardly any week where it hasn’t happened at least once.\n\nI mean, not always exactly the same way each time. Sometimes it’s one of those private eating spells and then I make myself sick afterwards. Other times I don’t do that, but I’ll skip meals the next day or push myself too hard with the walking, telling myself I’m just being disciplined, when really I know I’m trying to make up for what I ate.\n\nBut if you’re asking how often it happens without me being sick afterwards specifically… it’s been fairly regular, mixed in with the other times. I suppose the honest answer is that the whole pattern has been at least weekly since the winter, one way or another.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, it’s not the only way. It’s the main one, I suppose, but there are other things I do as well, and I don’t always admit to myself that that’s what I’m doing.

The next day, if I’ve eaten too much the night before, I’ll often skip breakfast and then lunch as well. I’ll tell myself I’m just not hungry, or that I’m being sensible, but if I’m honest it’s more like I’m punishing myself or trying to cancel it out somehow.

And with walking too — I go with a walking group, and sometimes I’ll push myself to do extra laps or go further than I normally would. Not because I feel full of energy or because I’m enjoying it particularly, but because I’m thinking, right, you have to undo what you ate. It becomes very calculated in my head, even though I try to make it look normal.

Sometimes I don’t do that part, but I still try to sort of… compensate, I suppose.\n\nIf I’ve had one of those episodes in the evening, the next day I’ll tell myself I’m just “not hungry,” but really I’ll skip breakfast and lunch on purpose. I’ll have tea and keep busy at work or around the house so I don’t have to think about it. Or if it’s a walking-group day, I’ll push myself to do extra laps, more than I normally would, and it isn’t because I’m feeling full of energy or enjoying the walk. It’s very much because I’m thinking, I have to undo what I ate yesterday.\n\nIt sounds ridiculous when I say it, but at the time it feels almost like a rule I’ve made for myself. Like if I don’t do something the next day, then the food “counts,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can't stop eating or control how much you're eating during those episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s exactly the frightening part of it, really. It doesn’t feel like ordinary hunger, or like I’ve made a decision to have a treat and then another one. Once I start, it’s as if a switch flips.\n\nI can be standing there thinking, Linda, stop now, you’ve had enough, put it away, and at the very same time my hands are still reaching for more. It’s almost like I’m watching myself do it from a little distance, if that makes any sense. I know I don’t want to carry on, I know I’ll feel awful afterwards, and yet I keep opening packets or going back to the fridge or cutting another slice.\n\nI feel embarrassed saying that because I’m a grown woman, and in every other part of my life I’m quite capable and organised. But in those moments, no, I don’t feel in control of it. I feel like I should be able to stop, but I can’t seem to make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up the main way you try to undo the effects of eating too much, or do you use other methods, like laxatives or diuretics, as much or more often?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly the throwing up, yes. I don’t really… I mean, the thing that finally made me say something to someone was realising I’d got into this awful little routine after eating too much.\n\nI’ll go upstairs and make myself sick, and then come back down or sort of carry on as if I’ve just been tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed. It sounds so sneaky when I say it out loud, and I hate that, because I’m not a dishonest person generally. But I’ll make it look like I’m just doing normal evening things, putting plates away or going to brush my teeth, when actually I’ve gone off because I’m panicking about what I’ve eaten.\n\nSo yes… that’s the main way. It isn’t something I’m proud of at all.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes of eating a very large amount of food in a short time without then making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, really… I don’t think there’s been hardly any week where it hasn’t happened at least once.\n\nI mean, not always exactly the same way each time. Sometimes it’s one of those private eating spells and then I make myself sick afterwards. Other times I don’t do that, but I’ll skip meals the next day or push myself too hard with the walking, telling myself I’m just being disciplined, when really I know I’m trying to make up for what I ate.\n\nBut if you’re asking how often it happens without me being sick afterwards specifically… it’s been fairly regular, mixed in with the other times. I suppose the honest answer is that the whole pattern has been at least weekly since the winter, one way or another.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up the only way you try to compensate for eating too much, or are there other methods you use as well?\n\nPatient: No, it’s not the only way. It’s the main one, I suppose, but there are other things I do as well, and I don’t always admit to myself that that’s what I’m doing.\n\nThe next day, if I’ve eaten too much the night before, I’ll often skip breakfast and then lunch as well. I’ll tell myself I’m just not hungry, or that I’m being sensible, but if I’m honest it’s more like I’m punishing myself or trying to cancel it out somehow.\n\nAnd with walking too — I go with a walking group, and sometimes I’ll push myself to do extra laps or go further than I normally would. Not because I feel full of energy or because I’m enjoying it particularly, but because I’m thinking, right, you have to undo what you ate. It becomes very calculated in my head, even though I try to make it look normal.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

Yes… I suppose the other things are the skipping meals and the walking, really. I keep telling myself it’s not really happening in the way it sounds, or that I’ve got it under control, but the fact that I’m planning around it and pretending I’m doing something normal afterwards… that’s what frightened me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you eat an excessively large amount of food in a short period of time before making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It’s not just having a bit too much pudding or seconds at supper. It’s more like, if no one is in the kitchen and I know I won’t be interrupted, I can sort of… start picking, and then it becomes this whole thing.\n\nI might go through leftovers from a church supper, then crackers, cheese, cookies, and if I’ve baked something for book club, I’ll eat that too. All in one sitting. It’s far, far more than I would ever put on a plate and serve myself as a proper meal. I wouldn’t let anyone see me eat like that. I’d be mortified. But when I’m alone, it can happen very quickly, and then I feel like I have to undo it somehow.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, really… there’s hardly been a week when it hasn’t happened at least once. I mean, the throwing up specifically is often after one of those private eating spells, when I’ve eaten far more than I meant to and I panic. But sometimes I’ve tried to “make up for it” in other ways too, like not eating properly the next day or doing far too much walking, as if that somehow balances it out.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I can hear how calculated that sounds, but in the moment it feels like I’ve done something dreadful and I need to fix it. So… yes, at least weekly, since last winter.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these episodes of eating a very large amount of food in a short time without then making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Yes… not always the throwing up, no. Sometimes I don’t do that part, but I still try to sort of… compensate, I suppose.\n\nIf I’ve had one of those episodes in the evening, the next day I’ll tell myself I’m just “not hungry,” but really I’ll skip breakfast and lunch on purpose. I’ll have tea and keep busy at work or around the house so I don’t have to think about it. Or if it’s a walking-group day, I’ll push myself to do extra laps, more than I normally would, and it isn’t because I’m feeling full of energy or enjoying the walk. It’s very much because I’m thinking, I have to undo what I ate yesterday.\n\nIt sounds ridiculous when I say it, but at the time it feels almost like a rule I’ve made for myself. Like if I don’t do something the next day, then the food “counts,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can't stop eating or control how much you're eating during those episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s exactly the frightening part of it, really. It doesn’t feel like ordinary hunger, or like I’ve made a decision to have a treat and then another one. Once I start, it’s as if a switch flips.\n\nI can be standing there thinking, Linda, stop now, you’ve had enough, put it away, and at the very same time my hands are still reaching for more. It’s almost like I’m watching myself do it from a little distance, if that makes any sense. I know I don’t want to carry on, I know I’ll feel awful afterwards, and yet I keep opening packets or going back to the fridge or cutting another slice.\n\nI feel embarrassed saying that because I’m a grown woman, and in every other part of my life I’m quite capable and organised. But in those moments, no, I don’t feel in control of it. I feel like I should be able to stop, but I can’t seem to make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up the main way you try to undo the effects of eating too much, or do you use other methods, like laxatives or diuretics, as much or more often?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly the throwing up, yes. I don’t really… I mean, the thing that finally made me say something to someone was realising I’d got into this awful little routine after eating too much.\n\nI’ll go upstairs and make myself sick, and then come back down or sort of carry on as if I’ve just been tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed. It sounds so sneaky when I say it out loud, and I hate that, because I’m not a dishonest person generally. But I’ll make it look like I’m just doing normal evening things, putting plates away or going to brush my teeth, when actually I’ve gone off because I’m panicking about what I’ve eaten.\n\nSo yes… that’s the main way. It isn’t something I’m proud of at all.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes of eating a very large amount of food in a short time without then making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, really… I don’t think there’s been hardly any week where it hasn’t happened at least once.\n\nI mean, not always exactly the same way each time. Sometimes it’s one of those private eating spells and then I make myself sick afterwards. Other times I don’t do that, but I’ll skip meals the next day or push myself too hard with the walking, telling myself I’m just being disciplined, when really I know I’m trying to make up for what I ate.\n\nBut if you’re asking how often it happens without me being sick afterwards specifically… it’s been fairly regular, mixed in with the other times. I suppose the honest answer is that the whole pattern has been at least weekly since the winter, one way or another.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up the only way you try to compensate for eating too much, or are there other methods you use as well?\n\nPatient: No, it’s not the only way. It’s the main one, I suppose, but there are other things I do as well, and I don’t always admit to myself that that’s what I’m doing.\n\nThe next day, if I’ve eaten too much the night before, I’ll often skip breakfast and then lunch as well. I’ll tell myself I’m just not hungry, or that I’m being sensible, but if I’m honest it’s more like I’m punishing myself or trying to cancel it out somehow.\n\nAnd with walking too — I go with a walking group, and sometimes I’ll push myself to do extra laps or go further than I normally would. Not because I feel full of energy or because I’m enjoying it particularly, but because I’m thinking, right, you have to undo what you ate. It becomes very calculated in my head, even though I try to make it look normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you use any other methods besides throwing up or restricting your food intake to try to compensate for eating too much?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose the other things are the skipping meals and the walking, really. I know you mentioned restricting food, so maybe that’s part of what you mean, but for me it tends to be the next day — I’ll miss breakfast and then lunch, and I’ll tell myself I’m just “getting back on track,” but honestly it’s because I’m trying to undo what I ate.\n\nAnd with the walking group, I’ll push myself to do extra laps or go further than I normally would. It’s not because I feel energetic or because I’m suddenly very keen on fitness. It’s more like I’ve got this running tally in my head, and I feel I have to make up for it somehow. I make it look quite ordinary, like I’m just being enthusiastic, but I know what I’m really doing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I’m not sure I can give you a clean number for that, because most of the time if I do have one of those episodes, I end up doing something afterwards — even if it isn’t straight away or even if I dress it up as something else in my head.

If you mean absolutely nothing at all afterwards, no being sick, no cutting back, no extra exercise, then… I’d have to think. I don’t really notice many times like that, because once it’s happened I usually get very anxious and start thinking about how to put it right somehow. So I can’t honestly say how often that happens. It might happen occasionally, but it’s not something I’ve kept track of in the same way.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I honestly don’t know. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very neat answer, but it’s hard for me to separate it out like that.

If I eat a large amount in a short space of time, I usually don’t just… leave it alone afterwards, if that makes sense. Even if I don’t do something immediately, my head starts going over it — what I’ve eaten, what I “should” do next, how I’m going to fix it. So the times when I do absolutely nothing afterwards are hard for me to identify.

There might be the odd time where nothing obvious happens, but I don’t really keep track of those in the same way. I’m much more aware of the times where I feel panicky afterwards and then try to make up for it somehow. So I can’t really give you a frequency — I’m not sure I’ve noticed it clearly enough.

But in those moments, no, I don’t feel in control of it. I feel like I should be able to stop, but I can’t seem to make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up the main way you try to undo the effects of eating too much, or do you use other methods, like laxatives or diuretics, as much or more often?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly the throwing up, yes. I don’t really… I mean, the thing that finally made me say something to someone was realising I’d got into this awful little routine after eating too much.\n\nI’ll go upstairs and make myself sick, and then come back down or sort of carry on as if I’ve just been tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed. It sounds so sneaky when I say it out loud, and I hate that, because I’m not a dishonest person generally. But I’ll make it look like I’m just doing normal evening things, putting plates away or going to brush my teeth, when actually I’ve gone off because I’m panicking about what I’ve eaten.\n\nSo yes… that’s the main way. It isn’t something I’m proud of at all.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes of eating a very large amount of food in a short time without then making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, really… I don’t think there’s been hardly any week where it hasn’t happened at least once.\n\nI mean, not always exactly the same way each time. Sometimes it’s one of those private eating spells and then I make myself sick afterwards. Other times I don’t do that, but I’ll skip meals the next day or push myself too hard with the walking, telling myself I’m just being disciplined, when really I know I’m trying to make up for what I ate.\n\nBut if you’re asking how often it happens without me being sick afterwards specifically… it’s been fairly regular, mixed in with the other times. I suppose the honest answer is that the whole pattern has been at least weekly since the winter, one way or another.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up the only way you try to compensate for eating too much, or are there other methods you use as well?\n\nPatient: No, it’s not the only way. It’s the main one, I suppose, but there are other things I do as well, and I don’t always admit to myself that that’s what I’m doing.\n\nThe next day, if I’ve eaten too much the night before, I’ll often skip breakfast and then lunch as well. I’ll tell myself I’m just not hungry, or that I’m being sensible, but if I’m honest it’s more like I’m punishing myself or trying to cancel it out somehow.\n\nAnd with walking too — I go with a walking group, and sometimes I’ll push myself to do extra laps or go further than I normally would. Not because I feel full of energy or because I’m enjoying it particularly, but because I’m thinking, right, you have to undo what you ate. It becomes very calculated in my head, even though I try to make it look normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you use any other methods besides throwing up or restricting your food intake to try to compensate for eating too much?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose the other things are the skipping meals and the walking, really. I know you mentioned restricting food, so maybe that’s part of what you mean, but for me it tends to be the next day — I’ll miss breakfast and then lunch, and I’ll tell myself I’m just “getting back on track,” but honestly it’s because I’m trying to undo what I ate.\n\nAnd with the walking group, I’ll push myself to do extra laps or go further than I normally would. It’s not because I feel energetic or because I’m suddenly very keen on fitness. It’s more like I’ve got this running tally in my head, and I feel I have to make up for it somehow. I make it look quite ordinary, like I’m just being enthusiastic, but I know what I’m really doing.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes of eating a very large amount of food in a short time without using any method to compensate for it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean number for that, because most of the time if I do have one of those episodes, I end up doing something afterwards — even if it isn’t straight away or even if I dress it up as something else in my head.\n\nIf you mean absolutely nothing at all afterwards, no being sick, no cutting back, no extra exercise, then… I’d have to think. I don’t really notice many times like that, because once it’s happened I usually get very anxious and start thinking about how to put it right somehow. So I can’t honestly say how often that happens. It might happen occasionally, but it’s not something I’ve kept track of in the same way.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes of eating a very large amount of food in a short time without doing anything to compensate for it afterwards?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very neat answer, but it’s hard for me to separate it out like that.\n\nIf I eat a large amount in a short space of time, I usually don’t just… leave it alone afterwards, if that makes sense. Even if I don’t do something immediately, my head starts going over it — what I’ve eaten, what I “should” do next, how I’m going to fix it. So the times when I do absolutely nothing afterwards are hard for me to identify.\n\nThere might be the odd time where nothing obvious happens, but I don’t really keep track of those in the same way. I’m much more aware of the times where I feel panicky afterwards and then try to make up for it somehow. So I can’t really give you a frequency — I’m not sure I’ve noticed it clearly enough.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I really can’t estimate it properly, I’m afraid. I’ve been trying to think of the past month in my head, almost going week by week, but the difficulty is that I don’t really mark those occasions unless I do something afterwards.

If there was a time where I ate a lot and then truly did nothing — no sort of “making up for it” in any way — it doesn’t stand out to me. The ones that stay in my mind are the ones where I feel I need to respond to it somehow, because that’s when I get caught up thinking about it.

So I don’t want to invent a number just to be helpful. It might have happened once or twice, or it might not have happened at all in the strict sense you mean. I’m honestly not sure.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I suppose… what finally made me say something is that after I eat too much, I’ve been going upstairs and making myself throw up. And then I come back down or I sort of busy myself as if I’m just tidying the kitchen, or I’ll say I’m getting ready for bed, and it’s… it’s become this awful little routine that I’m trying to hide.\n\nI feel quite ashamed saying it out loud, honestly. I keep telling myself it’s not really happening in the way it sounds, or that I’ve got it under control, but the fact that I’m planning around it and pretending I’m doing something normal afterwards… that’s what frightened me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you eat an excessively large amount of food in a short period of time before making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It’s not just having a bit too much pudding or seconds at supper. It’s more like, if no one is in the kitchen and I know I won’t be interrupted, I can sort of… start picking, and then it becomes this whole thing.\n\nI might go through leftovers from a church supper, then crackers, cheese, cookies, and if I’ve baked something for book club, I’ll eat that too. All in one sitting. It’s far, far more than I would ever put on a plate and serve myself as a proper meal. I wouldn’t let anyone see me eat like that. I’d be mortified. But when I’m alone, it can happen very quickly, and then I feel like I have to undo it somehow.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, really… there’s hardly been a week when it hasn’t happened at least once. I mean, the throwing up specifically is often after one of those private eating spells, when I’ve eaten far more than I meant to and I panic. But sometimes I’ve tried to “make up for it” in other ways too, like not eating properly the next day or doing far too much walking, as if that somehow balances it out.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I can hear how calculated that sounds, but in the moment it feels like I’ve done something dreadful and I need to fix it. So… yes, at least weekly, since last winter.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these episodes of eating a very large amount of food in a short time without then making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Yes… not always the throwing up, no. Sometimes I don’t do that part, but I still try to sort of… compensate, I suppose.\n\nIf I’ve had one of those episodes in the evening, the next day I’ll tell myself I’m just “not hungry,” but really I’ll skip breakfast and lunch on purpose. I’ll have tea and keep busy at work or around the house so I don’t have to think about it. Or if it’s a walking-group day, I’ll push myself to do extra laps, more than I normally would, and it isn’t because I’m feeling full of energy or enjoying the walk. It’s very much because I’m thinking, I have to undo what I ate yesterday.\n\nIt sounds ridiculous when I say it, but at the time it feels almost like a rule I’ve made for myself. Like if I don’t do something the next day, then the food “counts,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can't stop eating or control how much you're eating during those episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s exactly the frightening part of it, really. It doesn’t feel like ordinary hunger, or like I’ve made a decision to have a treat and then another one. Once I start, it’s as if a switch flips.\n\nI can be standing there thinking, Linda, stop now, you’ve had enough, put it away, and at the very same time my hands are still reaching for more. It’s almost like I’m watching myself do it from a little distance, if that makes any sense. I know I don’t want to carry on, I know I’ll feel awful afterwards, and yet I keep opening packets or going back to the fridge or cutting another slice.\n\nI feel embarrassed saying that because I’m a grown woman, and in every other part of my life I’m quite capable and organised. But in those moments, no, I don’t feel in control of it. I feel like I should be able to stop, but I can’t seem to make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up the main way you try to undo the effects of eating too much, or do you use other methods, like laxatives or diuretics, as much or more often?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly the throwing up, yes. I don’t really… I mean, the thing that finally made me say something to someone was realising I’d got into this awful little routine after eating too much.\n\nI’ll go upstairs and make myself sick, and then come back down or sort of carry on as if I’ve just been tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed. It sounds so sneaky when I say it out loud, and I hate that, because I’m not a dishonest person generally. But I’ll make it look like I’m just doing normal evening things, putting plates away or going to brush my teeth, when actually I’ve gone off because I’m panicking about what I’ve eaten.\n\nSo yes… that’s the main way. It isn’t something I’m proud of at all.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes of eating a very large amount of food in a short time without then making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, really… I don’t think there’s been hardly any week where it hasn’t happened at least once.\n\nI mean, not always exactly the same way each time. Sometimes it’s one of those private eating spells and then I make myself sick afterwards. Other times I don’t do that, but I’ll skip meals the next day or push myself too hard with the walking, telling myself I’m just being disciplined, when really I know I’m trying to make up for what I ate.\n\nBut if you’re asking how often it happens without me being sick afterwards specifically… it’s been fairly regular, mixed in with the other times. I suppose the honest answer is that the whole pattern has been at least weekly since the winter, one way or another.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up the only way you try to compensate for eating too much, or are there other methods you use as well?\n\nPatient: No, it’s not the only way. It’s the main one, I suppose, but there are other things I do as well, and I don’t always admit to myself that that’s what I’m doing.\n\nThe next day, if I’ve eaten too much the night before, I’ll often skip breakfast and then lunch as well. I’ll tell myself I’m just not hungry, or that I’m being sensible, but if I’m honest it’s more like I’m punishing myself or trying to cancel it out somehow.\n\nAnd with walking too — I go with a walking group, and sometimes I’ll push myself to do extra laps or go further than I normally would. Not because I feel full of energy or because I’m enjoying it particularly, but because I’m thinking, right, you have to undo what you ate. It becomes very calculated in my head, even though I try to make it look normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you use any other methods besides throwing up or restricting your food intake to try to compensate for eating too much?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose the other things are the skipping meals and the walking, really. I know you mentioned restricting food, so maybe that’s part of what you mean, but for me it tends to be the next day — I’ll miss breakfast and then lunch, and I’ll tell myself I’m just “getting back on track,” but honestly it’s because I’m trying to undo what I ate.\n\nAnd with the walking group, I’ll push myself to do extra laps or go further than I normally would. It’s not because I feel energetic or because I’m suddenly very keen on fitness. It’s more like I’ve got this running tally in my head, and I feel I have to make up for it somehow. I make it look quite ordinary, like I’m just being enthusiastic, but I know what I’m really doing.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes of eating a very large amount of food in a short time without using any method to compensate for it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean number for that, because most of the time if I do have one of those episodes, I end up doing something afterwards — even if it isn’t straight away or even if I dress it up as something else in my head.\n\nIf you mean absolutely nothing at all afterwards, no being sick, no cutting back, no extra exercise, then… I’d have to think. I don’t really notice many times like that, because once it’s happened I usually get very anxious and start thinking about how to put it right somehow. So I can’t honestly say how often that happens. It might happen occasionally, but it’s not something I’ve kept track of in the same way.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes of eating a very large amount of food in a short time without doing anything to compensate for it afterwards?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very neat answer, but it’s hard for me to separate it out like that.\n\nIf I eat a large amount in a short space of time, I usually don’t just… leave it alone afterwards, if that makes sense. Even if I don’t do something immediately, my head starts going over it — what I’ve eaten, what I “should” do next, how I’m going to fix it. So the times when I do absolutely nothing afterwards are hard for me to identify.\n\nThere might be the odd time where nothing obvious happens, but I don’t really keep track of those in the same way. I’m much more aware of the times where I feel panicky afterwards and then try to make up for it somehow. So I can’t really give you a frequency — I’m not sure I’ve noticed it clearly enough.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how many times in the past month you've eaten a very large amount of food in a short time and then done nothing at all to compensate for it?\n\nPatient: I really can’t estimate it properly, I’m afraid. I’ve been trying to think of the past month in my head, almost going week by week, but the difficulty is that I don’t really mark those occasions unless I do something afterwards.\n\nIf there was a time where I ate a lot and then truly did nothing — no sort of “making up for it” in any way — it doesn’t stand out to me. The ones that stay in my mind are the ones where I feel I need to respond to it somehow, because that’s when I get caught up thinking about it.\n\nSo I don’t want to invent a number just to be helpful. It might have happened once or twice, or it might not have happened at all in the strict sense you mean. I’m honestly not sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t really notice many times like that, because once it’s happened I usually get very anxious and start thinking about how to put it right somehow. So I can’t honestly say how often that happens. It might happen occasionally, but it’s not something I’ve kept track of in the same way.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes of eating a very large amount of food in a short time without doing anything to compensate for it afterwards?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very neat answer, but it’s hard for me to separate it out like that.\n\nIf I eat a large amount in a short space of time, I usually don’t just… leave it alone afterwards, if that makes sense. Even if I don’t do something immediately, my head starts going over it — what I’ve eaten, what I “should” do next, how I’m going to fix it. So the times when I do absolutely nothing afterwards are hard for me to identify.\n\nThere might be the odd time where nothing obvious happens, but I don’t really keep track of those in the same way. I’m much more aware of the times where I feel panicky afterwards and then try to make up for it somehow. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I think it is. That’s the one I’ve been doing most, and it’s honestly the part that made me realise I couldn’t just keep pretending it was all under control.

After I’ve eaten too much, I’ll often go upstairs and make myself throw up. And then I’ll sort of cover it over, like I’m just going up to get ready for bed, or I’ll come back down and act as if I’ve been tidying the kitchen or sorting something out. It sounds awful saying it plainly like that, but that’s what’s been happening.

I don’t always admit it to myself at the time. I’ll tell myself I just need a minute, or I feel too full, but really I know what I’m going to do. That’s why I finally said something.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I suppose… what finally made me say something is that after I eat too much, I’ve been going upstairs and making myself throw up. And then I come back down or I sort of busy myself as if I’m just tidying the kitchen, or I’ll say I’m getting ready for bed, and it’s… it’s become this awful little routine that I’m trying to hide.\n\nI feel quite ashamed saying it out loud, honestly. I keep telling myself it’s not really happening in the way it sounds, or that I’ve got it under control, but the fact that I’m planning around it and pretending I’m doing something normal afterwards… that’s what frightened me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you eat an excessively large amount of food in a short period of time before making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It’s not just having a bit too much pudding or seconds at supper. It’s more like, if no one is in the kitchen and I know I won’t be interrupted, I can sort of… start picking, and then it becomes this whole thing.\n\nI might go through leftovers from a church supper, then crackers, cheese, cookies, and if I’ve baked something for book club, I’ll eat that too. All in one sitting. It’s far, far more than I would ever put on a plate and serve myself as a proper meal. I wouldn’t let anyone see me eat like that. I’d be mortified. But when I’m alone, it can happen very quickly, and then I feel like I have to undo it somehow.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, really… there’s hardly been a week when it hasn’t happened at least once. I mean, the throwing up specifically is often after one of those private eating spells, when I’ve eaten far more than I meant to and I panic. But sometimes I’ve tried to “make up for it” in other ways too, like not eating properly the next day or doing far too much walking, as if that somehow balances it out.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I can hear how calculated that sounds, but in the moment it feels like I’ve done something dreadful and I need to fix it. So… yes, at least weekly, since last winter.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these episodes of eating a very large amount of food in a short time without then making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Yes… not always the throwing up, no. Sometimes I don’t do that part, but I still try to sort of… compensate, I suppose.\n\nIf I’ve had one of those episodes in the evening, the next day I’ll tell myself I’m just “not hungry,” but really I’ll skip breakfast and lunch on purpose. I’ll have tea and keep busy at work or around the house so I don’t have to think about it. Or if it’s a walking-group day, I’ll push myself to do extra laps, more than I normally would, and it isn’t because I’m feeling full of energy or enjoying the walk. It’s very much because I’m thinking, I have to undo what I ate yesterday.\n\nIt sounds ridiculous when I say it, but at the time it feels almost like a rule I’ve made for myself. Like if I don’t do something the next day, then the food “counts,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can't stop eating or control how much you're eating during those episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s exactly the frightening part of it, really. It doesn’t feel like ordinary hunger, or like I’ve made a decision to have a treat and then another one. Once I start, it’s as if a switch flips.\n\nI can be standing there thinking, Linda, stop now, you’ve had enough, put it away, and at the very same time my hands are still reaching for more. It’s almost like I’m watching myself do it from a little distance, if that makes any sense. I know I don’t want to carry on, I know I’ll feel awful afterwards, and yet I keep opening packets or going back to the fridge or cutting another slice.\n\nI feel embarrassed saying that because I’m a grown woman, and in every other part of my life I’m quite capable and organised. But in those moments, no, I don’t feel in control of it. I feel like I should be able to stop, but I can’t seem to make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up the main way you try to undo the effects of eating too much, or do you use other methods, like laxatives or diuretics, as much or more often?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly the throwing up, yes. I don’t really… I mean, the thing that finally made me say something to someone was realising I’d got into this awful little routine after eating too much.\n\nI’ll go upstairs and make myself sick, and then come back down or sort of carry on as if I’ve just been tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed. It sounds so sneaky when I say it out loud, and I hate that, because I’m not a dishonest person generally. But I’ll make it look like I’m just doing normal evening things, putting plates away or going to brush my teeth, when actually I’ve gone off because I’m panicking about what I’ve eaten.\n\nSo yes… that’s the main way. It isn’t something I’m proud of at all.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes of eating a very large amount of food in a short time without then making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, really… I don’t think there’s been hardly any week where it hasn’t happened at least once.\n\nI mean, not always exactly the same way each time. Sometimes it’s one of those private eating spells and then I make myself sick afterwards. Other times I don’t do that, but I’ll skip meals the next day or push myself too hard with the walking, telling myself I’m just being disciplined, when really I know I’m trying to make up for what I ate.\n\nBut if you’re asking how often it happens without me being sick afterwards specifically… it’s been fairly regular, mixed in with the other times. I suppose the honest answer is that the whole pattern has been at least weekly since the winter, one way or another.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up the only way you try to compensate for eating too much, or are there other methods you use as well?\n\nPatient: No, it’s not the only way. It’s the main one, I suppose, but there are other things I do as well, and I don’t always admit to myself that that’s what I’m doing.\n\nThe next day, if I’ve eaten too much the night before, I’ll often skip breakfast and then lunch as well. I’ll tell myself I’m just not hungry, or that I’m being sensible, but if I’m honest it’s more like I’m punishing myself or trying to cancel it out somehow.\n\nAnd with walking too — I go with a walking group, and sometimes I’ll push myself to do extra laps or go further than I normally would. Not because I feel full of energy or because I’m enjoying it particularly, but because I’m thinking, right, you have to undo what you ate. It becomes very calculated in my head, even though I try to make it look normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you use any other methods besides throwing up or restricting your food intake to try to compensate for eating too much?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose the other things are the skipping meals and the walking, really. I know you mentioned restricting food, so maybe that’s part of what you mean, but for me it tends to be the next day — I’ll miss breakfast and then lunch, and I’ll tell myself I’m just “getting back on track,” but honestly it’s because I’m trying to undo what I ate.\n\nAnd with the walking group, I’ll push myself to do extra laps or go further than I normally would. It’s not because I feel energetic or because I’m suddenly very keen on fitness. It’s more like I’ve got this running tally in my head, and I feel I have to make up for it somehow. I make it look quite ordinary, like I’m just being enthusiastic, but I know what I’m really doing.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes of eating a very large amount of food in a short time without using any method to compensate for it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean number for that, because most of the time if I do have one of those episodes, I end up doing something afterwards — even if it isn’t straight away or even if I dress it up as something else in my head.\n\nIf you mean absolutely nothing at all afterwards, no being sick, no cutting back, no extra exercise, then… I’d have to think. I don’t really notice many times like that, because once it’s happened I usually get very anxious and start thinking about how to put it right somehow. So I can’t honestly say how often that happens. It might happen occasionally, but it’s not something I’ve kept track of in the same way.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes of eating a very large amount of food in a short time without doing anything to compensate for it afterwards?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. I’m trying to think of one clearly, but honestly… no, I can’t bring a definite one to mind.

The difficulty is that “nothing at all” is quite a strict way of putting it, isn’t it? I haven’t noticed an instance like that clearly enough to say yes.

And then I come back down or I sort of busy myself as if I’m just tidying the kitchen, or I’ll say I’m getting ready for bed, and it’s… it’s become this awful little routine that I’m trying to hide.\n\nI feel quite ashamed saying it out loud, honestly. I keep telling myself it’s not really happening in the way it sounds, or that I’ve got it under control, but the fact that I’m planning around it and pretending I’m doing something normal afterwards… that’s what frightened me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you eat an excessively large amount of food in a short period of time before making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It’s not just having a bit too much pudding or seconds at supper. It’s more like, if no one is in the kitchen and I know I won’t be interrupted, I can sort of… start picking, and then it becomes this whole thing.\n\nI might go through leftovers from a church supper, then crackers, cheese, cookies, and if I’ve baked something for book club, I’ll eat that too. All in one sitting. It’s far, far more than I would ever put on a plate and serve myself as a proper meal. I wouldn’t let anyone see me eat like that. I’d be mortified. But when I’m alone, it can happen very quickly, and then I feel like I have to undo it somehow.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, really… there’s hardly been a week when it hasn’t happened at least once. So… yes, at least weekly, since last winter.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these episodes of eating a very large amount of food in a short time without then making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Yes… not always the throwing up, no. Sometimes I don’t do that part, but I still try to sort of… compensate, I suppose.\n\nIf I’ve had one of those episodes in the evening, the next day I’ll tell myself I’m just “not hungry,” but really I’ll skip breakfast and lunch on purpose. I’ll have tea and keep busy at work or around the house so I don’t have to think about it. Or if it’s a walking-group day, I’ll push myself to do extra laps, more than I normally would, and it isn’t because I’m feeling full of energy or enjoying the walk. It’s very much because I’m thinking, I have to undo what I ate yesterday.\n\nIt sounds ridiculous when I say it, but at the time it feels almost like a rule I’ve made for myself. Like if I don’t do something the next day, then the food “counts,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can't stop eating or control how much you're eating during those episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s exactly the frightening part of it, really. It doesn’t feel like ordinary hunger, or like I’ve made a decision to have a treat and then another one. Once I start, it’s as if a switch flips.\n\nI can be standing there thinking, Linda, stop now, you’ve had enough, put it away, and at the very same time my hands are still reaching for more. It’s almost like I’m watching myself do it from a little distance, if that makes any sense. I know I don’t want to carry on, I know I’ll feel awful afterwards, and yet I keep opening packets or going back to the fridge or cutting another slice.\n\nI feel embarrassed saying that because I’m a grown woman, and in every other part of my life I’m quite capable and organised. But in those moments, no, I don’t feel in control of it. I feel like I should be able to stop, but I can’t seem to make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up the main way you try to undo the effects of eating too much, or do you use other methods, like laxatives or diuretics, as much or more often?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly the throwing up, yes. I don’t really… I mean, the thing that finally made me say something to someone was realising I’d got into this awful little routine after eating too much.\n\nI’ll go upstairs and make myself sick, and then come back down or sort of carry on as if I’ve just been tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed. It sounds so sneaky when I say it out loud, and I hate that, because I’m not a dishonest person generally. But I’ll make it look like I’m just doing normal evening things, putting plates away or going to brush my teeth, when actually I’ve gone off because I’m panicking about what I’ve eaten.\n\nSo yes… that’s the main way. It isn’t something I’m proud of at all.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes of eating a very large amount of food in a short time without then making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, really… I don’t think there’s been hardly any week where it hasn’t happened at least once.\n\nI mean, not always exactly the same way each time. Sometimes it’s one of those private eating spells and then I make myself sick afterwards. Other times I don’t do that, but I’ll skip meals the next day or push myself too hard with the walking, telling myself I’m just being disciplined, when really I know I’m trying to make up for what I ate.\n\nBut if you’re asking how often it happens without me being sick afterwards specifically… it’s been fairly regular, mixed in with the other times. I suppose the honest answer is that the whole pattern has been at least weekly since the winter, one way or another.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up the only way you try to compensate for eating too much, or are there other methods you use as well?\n\nPatient: No, it’s not the only way. It’s the main one, I suppose, but there are other things I do as well, and I don’t always admit to myself that that’s what I’m doing.\n\nThe next day, if I’ve eaten too much the night before, I’ll often skip breakfast and then lunch as well. I’ll tell myself I’m just not hungry, or that I’m being sensible, but if I’m honest it’s more like I’m punishing myself or trying to cancel it out somehow.\n\nAnd with walking too — I go with a walking group, and sometimes I’ll push myself to do extra laps or go further than I normally would. Not because I feel full of energy or because I’m enjoying it particularly, but because I’m thinking, right, you have to undo what you ate. It becomes very calculated in my head, even though I try to make it look normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you use any other methods besides throwing up or restricting your food intake to try to compensate for eating too much?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose the other things are the skipping meals and the walking, really. I know you mentioned restricting food, so maybe that’s part of what you mean, but for me it tends to be the next day — I’ll miss breakfast and then lunch, and I’ll tell myself I’m just “getting back on track,” but honestly it’s because I’m trying to undo what I ate.\n\nAnd with the walking group, I’ll push myself to do extra laps or go further than I normally would. It’s not because I feel energetic or because I’m suddenly very keen on fitness. It’s more like I’ve got this running tally in my head, and I feel I have to make up for it somehow. I make it look quite ordinary, like I’m just being enthusiastic, but I know what I’m really doing.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes of eating a very large amount of food in a short time without using any method to compensate for it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean number for that, because most of the time if I do have one of those episodes, I end up doing something afterwards — even if it isn’t straight away or even if I dress it up as something else in my head.\n\nIf you mean absolutely nothing at all afterwards, no being sick, no cutting back, no extra exercise, then… I’d have to think. I don’t really notice many times like that, because once it’s happened I usually get very anxious and start thinking about how to put it right somehow. So I can’t honestly say how often that happens. It might happen occasionally, but it’s not something I’ve kept track of in the same way.\n\nDoctor: How often do you have episodes of eating a very large amount of food in a short time without doing anything to compensate for it afterwards?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very neat answer, but it’s hard for me to separate it out like that.\n\nIf I eat a large amount in a short space of time, I usually don’t just… leave it alone afterwards, if that makes sense. Even if I don’t do something immediately, my head starts going over it — what I’ve eaten, what I “should” do next, how I’m going to fix it. So the times when I do absolutely nothing afterwards are hard for me to identify.\n\nThere might be the odd time where nothing obvious happens, but I don’t really keep track of those in the same way. I’m much more aware of the times where I feel panicky afterwards and then try to make up for it somehow. So I can’t really give you a frequency — I’m not sure I’ve noticed it clearly enough.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how many times in the past month you've eaten a very large amount of food in a short time and then done nothing at all to compensate for it?\n\nPatient: I really can’t estimate it properly, I’m afraid. I’ve been trying to think of the past month in my head, almost going week by week, but the difficulty is that I don’t really mark those occasions unless I do something afterwards.\n\nIf there was a time where I ate a lot and then truly did nothing — no sort of “making up for it” in any way — it doesn’t stand out to me. The ones that stay in my mind are the ones where I feel I need to respond to it somehow, because that’s when I get caught up thinking about it.\n\nSo I don’t want to invent a number just to be helpful. It might have happened once or twice, or it might not have happened at all in the strict sense you mean. I’m honestly not sure.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself throw up the method you use most often to try to compensate for eating too much?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it is. That’s the one I’ve been doing most, and it’s honestly the part that made me realise I couldn’t just keep pretending it was all under control.\n\nAfter I’ve eaten too much, I’ll often go upstairs and make myself throw up. And then I’ll sort of cover it over, like I’m just going up to get ready for bed, or I’ll come back down and act as if I’ve been tidying the kitchen or sorting something out. It sounds awful saying it plainly like that, but that’s what’s been happening.\n\nI don’t always admit it to myself at the time. I’ll tell myself I just need a minute, or I feel too full, but really I know what I’m going to do. That’s why I finally said something.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any instance in the past month where you ate a very large amount of food in a short time and did nothing at all to compensate for it afterwards, not even skipping a meal or exercising more?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one clearly, but honestly… no, I can’t bring a definite one to mind.\n\nThe difficulty is that “nothing at all” is quite a strict way of putting it, isn’t it? Because even if I didn’t make myself sick afterwards, I might still have eaten less later, or told myself I’d be “better” the next day, or walked more, or sort of adjusted things without really counting it as compensating at the time. It’s only when you say it like that — not even skipping a meal or exercising more — that I realise I don’t know how often I’ve ever just let it be.\n\nIn the past month, I can remember times I ate too much and then felt that horrible panic and shame afterwards, but I can’t confidently say, “Yes, that time I did absolutely nothing afterwards.” I don’t want to give you a false answer just to sound certain. Yes… I think so. I mean, if I’m honest, it has been going on that often.

Since last winter, really, there’s hardly been a week where I haven’t had at least one of those private episodes where I eat far too much in a short space of time and then feel I have to undo it somehow. Most often that’s making myself throw up, but if I don’t do that, I’ll skip food afterwards or the next day, or I’ll push myself to walk much more than I normally would.

Twice a week… I’m trying not to overstate it, but yes, there have definitely been many weeks where it’s been at least that. It’s not just a one-off bad week here and there. It’s been a regular pattern for months, which is horrible to admit out loud.
